People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. I also check photos of the layout over and over until it feels “settled.” Part of me knows it’s probably already fine, but I still get stuck doing it and lose a lot of studio time.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre these thoughts and behaviors interfering with your daily life or relationships outside of your studio work?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 32 / artist_or_related_worker / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I’m still getting most of my work done, but the extra time I lose in the studio already cost me a grant deadline and made me back out of a gallery talk I had actually wanted to do.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

It’s more that the thoughts come back, and then I get caught up in them.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you have any recurring, unwanted thoughts or compulsions to perform specific rituals outside of your studio work?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 32 / artist_or_related_worker / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. It’s more that the thoughts come back, and then I get caught up in them.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring, unwanted thoughts or compulsions to perform specific rituals outside of your studio work?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve noticed, no. I don’t think I have specific rituals outside of the studio. If there are unwanted thoughts, they mostly seem tied to the work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious in general, even when you're not thinking about the memorial piece or your work?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I haven’t noticed feeling anxious in general when my mind isn’t on that stuff. It seems more connected to when those thoughts come up.\n\nDoctor: How distressing do you find these unwanted thoughts and the need to repeat actions when they happen?\n\nPatient: It’s distressing, but not overwhelming, I guess. When it happens, I’ll get stuck rereading the label text, nudging the same objects on the table, or checking layout photos again and again until it feels “settled.” Part of me knows it’s already fine, which makes it frustrating, because I can see I’m just losing studio time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these unwanted thoughts and compulsive behaviors are only happening with the memorial piece or could they potentially happen with other projects or aspects of your life as well?\n\nPatient: It feels mostly tied to the memorial piece, at least that’s why I came in. When I was working on it, I kept having these awful thoughts that I might dishonor my aunt somehow—like if I chose the wrong photo placement or the wrong color, it meant I didn’t love her enough. I couldn’t just brush it off like normal creative second-guessing. I’m not sure if it could happen with other projects, but this one is where it really showed up.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. That’s what made it so hard to brush off.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the unwanted thoughts and compulsive behaviors would occur with a project that is emotionally significant to you but in a positive way?\n\nPatient: I think it might, if it had that same feeling of importance attached to it. Even if it was positive, I could see myself getting stuck on whether I was doing it “right.” With my aunt’s memorial piece, it was these awful thoughts that one wrong photo placement or color choice meant I was dishonoring her or that I didn’t love her enough. I couldn’t just treat it like normal creative doubt.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these unwanted thoughts and compulsive behaviors could happen with a project that holds no emotional significance to you, but still requires a high level of precision or perfection?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it would be the same. I do care about precision in my work, but what brought me in was different—it was with my aunt’s memorial piece. I kept having these awful, unwanted thoughts that if I put one photo in the wrong place or chose the wrong color, I’d be dishonoring her or it meant I didn’t love her enough. That felt way beyond normal perfectionism or creative doubt.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F41.1, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I’ve been having these really awful, unwanted thoughts while working on a memorial piece for my aunt. I keep worrying that if I place a photo wrong, or choose the wrong color, it somehow means I’m dishonoring her or that I didn’t love her enough. It feels beyond normal creative doubt, and I can’t really brush it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel the need to repeat certain actions or check things over and over to try and get rid of these unwanted thoughts about the memorial piece?\n\nPatient: Yes. I’ll reread the label text again and again, and keep nudging the same objects on the table, like moving them a little and then moving them back. I also check photos of the layout over and over until it feels “settled.” Part of me knows it’s probably already fine, but I still get stuck doing it and lose a lot of studio time.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and behaviors interfering with your daily life or relationships outside of your studio work?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is. I’m still getting most of my work done, but it’s taking extra time in the studio, and that’s already had consequences. I missed a grant deadline because I got stuck on this, and I also backed out of a gallery talk I had actually wanted to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're not working on the memorial piece or thinking about it?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed a lot of anxiety when I’m completely away from it, but I also feel like it’s been taking up a lot of space in my head. I guess I’d have to pay more attention to that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're not thinking about the memorial piece or your work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve noticed much anxiety when I’m truly not thinking about it, but it’s hard to separate because it tends to pop back into my mind.\n\nDoctor: Do you generally feel anxious when you're not thinking about your work or the memorial piece?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I generally feel anxious when I’m not thinking about it. I mean, I haven’t really noticed that. It’s more that the thoughts come back, and then I get caught up in them.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring, unwanted thoughts or compulsions to perform specific rituals outside of your studio work?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve noticed, no. I don’t think I have specific rituals outside of the studio. If there are unwanted thoughts, they mostly seem tied to the work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious in general, even when you're not thinking about the memorial piece or your work?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I haven’t noticed feeling anxious in general when my mind isn’t on that stuff. It seems more connected to when those thoughts come up.\n\nDoctor: How distressing do you find these unwanted thoughts and the need to repeat actions when they happen?\n\nPatient: It’s distressing, but not overwhelming, I guess. When it happens, I’ll get stuck rereading the label text, nudging the same objects on the table, or checking layout photos again and again until it feels “settled.” Part of me knows it’s already fine, which makes it frustrating, because I can see I’m just losing studio time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these unwanted thoughts and compulsive behaviors are only happening with the memorial piece or could they potentially happen with other projects or aspects of your life as well?\n\nPatient: It feels mostly tied to the memorial piece, at least that’s why I came in. When I was working on it, I kept having these awful thoughts that I might dishonor my aunt somehow—like if I chose the wrong photo placement or the wrong color, it meant I didn’t love her enough. I couldn’t just brush it off like normal creative second-guessing. I’m not sure if it could happen with other projects, but this one is where it really showed up.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you could have these unwanted thoughts and compulsive behaviors with projects that have no personal or emotional significance to you?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. The only time it’s felt like this was with my aunt’s memorial piece. That’s what made it so intense—the thought that one wrong photo placement or color choice could somehow dishonor her or mean I didn’t love her enough. With regular projects, I have normal doubts, but I haven’t noticed them turning into that same kind of awful, stuck feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you could have these unwanted thoughts and compulsive behaviors with other projects that hold emotional significance to you, similar to the memorial piece for your aunt?\n\nPatient: I think it could, yes—if the project had that same kind of emotional weight. With my aunt’s memorial piece, it wasn’t just normal creative doubt. I kept getting these awful unwanted thoughts that if I placed a photo wrong or picked the wrong color, I was somehow dishonoring her or proving I didn’t love her enough. That’s what made it so hard to brush off.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the unwanted thoughts and compulsive behaviors would occur with a project that is emotionally significant to you but in a positive way?\n\nPatient: I think it might, if it had that same feeling of importance attached to it. Even if it was positive, I could see myself getting stuck on whether I was doing it “right.” With my aunt’s memorial piece, it was these awful thoughts that one wrong photo placement or color choice meant I was dishonoring her or that I didn’t love her enough. I couldn’t just treat it like normal creative doubt.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these unwanted thoughts and compulsive behaviors could happen with a project that holds no emotional significance to you, but still requires a high level of precision or perfection?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it would be the same. I kept having these awful, unwanted thoughts that if I put one photo in the wrong place or chose the wrong color, I’d be dishonoring her or it meant I didn’t love her enough. That felt way beyond normal perfectionism or creative doubt.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you could experience these unwanted thoughts and compulsive behaviors in a situation that is emotionally significant but not related to fear of making a mistake or doing something wrong?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F41.1, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I’ve been having these really awful, unwanted thoughts while working on a memorial piece for my aunt. I keep worrying that if I place a photo wrong, or choose the wrong color, it somehow means I’m dishonoring her or that I didn’t love her enough. It feels beyond normal creative doubt, and I can’t really brush it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel the need to repeat certain actions or check things over and over to try and get rid of these unwanted thoughts about the memorial piece?\n\nPatient: Yes. I’ll reread the label text again and again, and keep nudging the same objects on the table, like moving them a little and then moving them back. I also check photos of the layout over and over until it feels “settled.” Part of me knows it’s probably already fine, but I still get stuck doing it and lose a lot of studio time.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and behaviors interfering with your daily life or relationships outside of your studio work?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is. I’m still getting most of my work done, but it’s taking extra time in the studio, and that’s already had consequences. I missed a grant deadline because I got stuck on this, and I also backed out of a gallery talk I had actually wanted to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're not working on the memorial piece or thinking about it?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed a lot of anxiety when I’m completely away from it, but I also feel like it’s been taking up a lot of space in my head. I guess I’d have to pay more attention to that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're not thinking about the memorial piece or your work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve noticed much anxiety when I’m truly not thinking about it, but it’s hard to separate because it tends to pop back into my mind.\n\nDoctor: Do you generally feel anxious when you're not thinking about your work or the memorial piece?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I generally feel anxious when I’m not thinking about it. I mean, I haven’t really noticed that. It’s more that the thoughts come back, and then I get caught up in them.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring, unwanted thoughts or compulsions to perform specific rituals outside of your studio work?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve noticed, no. I don’t think I have specific rituals outside of the studio. If there are unwanted thoughts, they mostly seem tied to the work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious in general, even when you're not thinking about the memorial piece or your work?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I haven’t noticed feeling anxious in general when my mind isn’t on that stuff. It seems more connected to when those thoughts come up.\n\nDoctor: How distressing do you find these unwanted thoughts and the need to repeat actions when they happen?\n\nPatient: It’s distressing, but not overwhelming, I guess. When it happens, I’ll get stuck rereading the label text, nudging the same objects on the table, or checking layout photos again and again until it feels “settled.” Part of me knows it’s already fine, which makes it frustrating, because I can see I’m just losing studio time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these unwanted thoughts and compulsive behaviors are only happening with the memorial piece or could they potentially happen with other projects or aspects of your life as well?\n\nPatient: It feels mostly tied to the memorial piece, at least that’s why I came in. When I was working on it, I kept having these awful thoughts that I might dishonor my aunt somehow—like if I chose the wrong photo placement or the wrong color, it meant I didn’t love her enough. I couldn’t just brush it off like normal creative second-guessing. I’m not sure if it could happen with other projects, but this one is where it really showed up.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you could have these unwanted thoughts and compulsive behaviors with projects that have no personal or emotional significance to you?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. The only time it’s felt like this was with my aunt’s memorial piece. That’s what made it so intense—the thought that one wrong photo placement or color choice could somehow dishonor her or mean I didn’t love her enough. With regular projects, I have normal doubts, but I haven’t noticed them turning into that same kind of awful, stuck feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you could have these unwanted thoughts and compulsive behaviors with other projects that hold emotional significance to you, similar to the memorial piece for your aunt?\n\nPatient: I think it could, yes—if the project had that same kind of emotional weight. With my aunt’s memorial piece, it wasn’t just normal creative doubt. I kept getting these awful unwanted thoughts that if I placed a photo wrong or picked the wrong color, I was somehow dishonoring her or proving I didn’t love her enough. That’s what made it so hard to brush off.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the unwanted thoughts and compulsive behaviors would occur with a project that is emotionally significant to you but in a positive way?\n\nPatient: I think it might, if it had that same feeling of importance attached to it. I couldn’t just treat it like normal creative doubt.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these unwanted thoughts and compulsive behaviors could happen with a project that holds no emotional significance to you, but still requires a high level of precision or perfection?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it would be the same. I do care about precision in my work, but what brought me in was different—it was with my aunt’s memorial piece. I kept having these awful, unwanted thoughts that if I put one photo in the wrong place or chose the wrong color, I’d be dishonoring her or it meant I didn’t love her enough. That felt way beyond normal perfectionism or creative doubt.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you could experience these unwanted thoughts and compulsive behaviors in a situation that is emotionally significant but not related to fear of making a mistake or doing something wrong?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. Like, I kept thinking one wrong photo placement or color choice would somehow dishonor her or mean I didn’t love her enough. It didn’t feel like normal creative doubt—I couldn’t just let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you could experience these unwanted thoughts and compulsive behaviors in situations that are not emotionally significant to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t really think so, at least not in the same way. I kept having these unwanted thoughts that I’d somehow dishonor her—like if I put a photo in the wrong spot or picked the wrong color, it meant I didn’t love her enough. I couldn’t just dismiss it like normal creative doubt.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you could experience these unwanted thoughts and compulsive behaviors in a situation that has no emotional significance to you, but still causes you some level of stress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: Maybe some stress would make me overthink, but what I came in for felt more tied to how much it mattered emotionally. With my aunt’s memorial piece, I kept getting these awful thoughts that if I placed a photo wrong or picked the wrong color, I’d be dishonoring her or proving I didn’t love her enough. I couldn’t just brush that off like normal creative doubt.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these unwanted thoughts and compulsive behaviors could happen in any other area of your life outside of your work on the memorial piece?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed it in other areas the same way. The main thing that brought me in was the memorial piece for my aunt. It’s more that the thoughts come back, and then I get caught up in them.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring, unwanted thoughts or compulsions to perform specific rituals outside of your studio work?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve noticed, no. I don’t think I have specific rituals outside of the studio. If there are unwanted thoughts, they mostly seem tied to the work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious in general, even when you're not thinking about the memorial piece or your work?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I haven’t noticed feeling anxious in general when my mind isn’t on that stuff. It seems more connected to when those thoughts come up.\n\nDoctor: How distressing do you find these unwanted thoughts and the need to repeat actions when they happen?\n\nPatient: It’s distressing, but not overwhelming, I guess. When it happens, I’ll get stuck rereading the label text, nudging the same objects on the table, or checking layout photos again and again until it feels “settled.” Part of me knows it’s already fine, which makes it frustrating, because I can see I’m just losing studio time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these unwanted thoughts and compulsive behaviors are only happening with the memorial piece or could they potentially happen with other projects or aspects of your life as well?\n\nPatient: It feels mostly tied to the memorial piece, at least that’s why I came in. When I was working on it, I kept having these awful thoughts that I might dishonor my aunt somehow—like if I chose the wrong photo placement or the wrong color, it meant I didn’t love her enough. I couldn’t just brush it off like normal creative second-guessing. I’m not sure if it could happen with other projects, but this one is where it really showed up.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you could have these unwanted thoughts and compulsive behaviors with projects that have no personal or emotional significance to you?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. The only time it’s felt like this was with my aunt’s memorial piece. That’s what made it so intense—the thought that one wrong photo placement or color choice could somehow dishonor her or mean I didn’t love her enough. With regular projects, I have normal doubts, but I haven’t noticed them turning into that same kind of awful, stuck feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you could have these unwanted thoughts and compulsive behaviors with other projects that hold emotional significance to you, similar to the memorial piece for your aunt?\n\nPatient: I think it could, yes—if the project had that same kind of emotional weight. With my aunt’s memorial piece, it wasn’t just normal creative doubt. I kept getting these awful unwanted thoughts that if I placed a photo wrong or picked the wrong color, I was somehow dishonoring her or proving I didn’t love her enough. That’s what made it so hard to brush off.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the unwanted thoughts and compulsive behaviors would occur with a project that is emotionally significant to you but in a positive way?\n\nPatient: I think it might, if it had that same feeling of importance attached to it. Even if it was positive, I could see myself getting stuck on whether I was doing it “right.” With my aunt’s memorial piece, it was these awful thoughts that one wrong photo placement or color choice meant I was dishonoring her or that I didn’t love her enough. I couldn’t just treat it like normal creative doubt.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these unwanted thoughts and compulsive behaviors could happen with a project that holds no emotional significance to you, but still requires a high level of precision or perfection?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it would be the same. That felt way beyond normal perfectionism or creative doubt.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you could experience these unwanted thoughts and compulsive behaviors in a situation that is emotionally significant but not related to fear of making a mistake or doing something wrong?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. Like, I kept thinking one wrong photo placement or color choice would somehow dishonor her or mean I didn’t love her enough. It didn’t feel like normal creative doubt—I couldn’t just let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you could experience these unwanted thoughts and compulsive behaviors in situations that are not emotionally significant to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t really think so, at least not in the same way. I kept having these unwanted thoughts that I’d somehow dishonor her—like if I put a photo in the wrong spot or picked the wrong color, it meant I didn’t love her enough. I couldn’t just dismiss it like normal creative doubt.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you could experience these unwanted thoughts and compulsive behaviors in a situation that has no emotional significance to you, but still causes you some level of stress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: Maybe some stress would make me overthink, but what I came in for felt more tied to how much it mattered emotionally. With my aunt’s memorial piece, I kept getting these awful thoughts that if I placed a photo wrong or picked the wrong color, I’d be dishonoring her or proving I didn’t love her enough. I couldn’t just brush that off like normal creative doubt.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these unwanted thoughts and compulsive behaviors could happen in any other area of your life outside of your work on the memorial piece?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed it in other areas the same way. I couldn’t just treat it like normal creative doubt.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these unwanted thoughts and compulsive behaviors could happen with a project that holds no emotional significance to you, but still requires a high level of precision or perfection?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it would be the same.
